After a particularly tense family argument in which her behavior frightened her daughter and she admitted she no longer felt like herself, her husband and pastor encouraged her to see their primary double shift instead of actually resting. \n- S032 (Death_Suicide): There have been moments, especially after a bad argument or when I mess up at work, where I catch myself thinking it might be easier if I just didn’t wake up, like my family would be better off without me, even though I haven’t made any plans to hurt myself. \n- S033 (Elevated_Irritable_Mood): I swing from feeling on top of the the only one who can fix everything in this family and at work, like I’ve got some special gift for seeing how it all fits together and everyone else is just too slow to keep up. \n- S036 (Decreased_Sleep): On my ‘up’ nights I can get by on three or four hours of broken sleep and still feel almost wired in the morning, like I don’t really need to rest even though my body is tired. \n- S037 (Pressured_Speech): My husband keeps telling me I’m talking a mile a minute, jumping from my mom’s doctor bills to my daughter’s ex to new ideas for the warehouse, and I feel this pressure inside like if I stop talking I’ll lose something important. \n- S038 (Racing_Thoughts): My thoughts won’t line up—they just race from one thing to another, like planning a new inventory system, then remembering a bill, then worrying about my mom dying, all piling on so fast I can’t grab hold of any one of them. \n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- delusions_and_hallucinations_occur_exclusively_during_the_manic_episode: Those voices calling my name or telling me I’m failing my family only ever show up when I’m in that wired, overdrive state, and After a particularly tense family argument in which her behavior frightened her daughter and she admitted she no longer felt like herself, her husband and pastor encouraged her to see their primary care doctor, who, concerned about the intensity and duration of her mood changes and their impact on home and work life, referred her urgently to psychiatry.\n- Chief Complaint: “My sleep has been nights now, especially when I’m really wired, I’ve heard my name being called clear as day from the hallway or a man’s voice whispering that I’m failing my family, but when I go check there’s nobody there and everyone else is asleep. \n- S024 (Depressed_Mood): Lately, when the energy drops, it’s like this heavy gray cloud settles over me and I walk around the house feeling useless and guilty, like no matter what I do I’m letting my daughter and my mom down. \n- S025 (Loss_of_Interest): I used to look forward to knitting and helping at the food pantry and even chatting with folks at church, Has it been going on for at least six months, or did it start more recently with your mood changes?\n\nPatient: No, it hasn't been six months at all. It started much more recently, about three months ago, right around the time my daughter had to move back in and my mother's health really started to slide.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of sadness, low energy, and loss of interest been present nearly every day, for most of the day, for at least the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes, those heavy, sad feelings and not caring about anything have been there nearly every single day, for most of the day. It's actually been going on much longer than two weeks—it's been about three months now, ever since my daughter's divorce and my mom's health started sliding. I just feel so completely worn down and overwhelmed by it all.\n\nDoctor: During this difficult time, have you had any thoughts of death, or felt that you would be better off dead?\n\nPatient: Yes, there have been moments, especially after a bad argument at home or when I mess up at work, where I catch myself thinking it might be easier if I just didn't wake up. I get to feeling like my family would be better off without me dragging everyone down. I want to be clear that I haven't made any plans to actually hurt myself, but those thoughts do cross my mind when things get really low.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods of time lasting several days where you felt the exact opposite of depressed—such as having unusually high energy, needing very little sleep, or feeling on top of the world?\n\nPatient: Yes, I definitely have those \"up\" periods, which have been happening on and off for about three months now. During those times, I feel on top of the world and convinced I can handle everything, but I also get so irritable that I snap at everyone while tearing around starting multiple projects. I can easily get by on just three or four hours of sleep and still feel completely wired the next day.\n\nDoctor: When you experience these 'up' periods of high energy and needing very little sleep, how many days in a row do they typically last?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure of the exact number of days because I tend to lose track of time when I'm in that wired state. But it definitely feels like those high-energy spells last for several days at a time before I finally crash.\n\nDoctor: During these high-energy periods, have you ever experienced severe problems at work or home, or have you ever needed to be hospitalized because of them?\n\nPatient: I haven't been hospitalized, but I've definitely had severe problems at work and at home. I was recently written up at the warehouse for making mistakes and missing shifts, and at home, my family is constantly tiptoeing around me because they don't know what to expect. They've even started taking over responsibilities I used to handle because of how unpredictable I've been.\n\nDoctor: During these high-energy periods, do you find yourself doing things that are risky or unusual for you, such as spending too much money, driving recklessly, or making impulsive decisions?\n\nPatient: I haven't really done specific things like driving recklessly or spending too much money. But I do get very carried away with starting a million projects at once and taking on too many tasks, like tearing around the house reorganizing everything.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling consistently depressed and low, or have you been experiencing a rapid mix of both high-energy and low-mood states happening at the same time?\n\nPatient: I don't really feel them at the exact same time, but rather I swing back and forth between the two. I'll go from being in a high-energy, wired overdrive where I feel on top of the world and take on everything, to suddenly crashing into a heavy gray cloud where I feel useless, guilty, and completely exhausted. These unpredictable ups and downs have been going on like this for about three months now.\n\nDoctor: During the periods when you feel depressed and down, how much does it affect your ability to do daily activities like work, chores, or taking care of yourself—is it very difficult but you still manage, or has it become almost impossible to do anything?\n\nPatient: It has become incredibly difficult, almost impossible at times when that heavy gray cloud is over me. I've been written up at the warehouse for mistakes and missed shifts, and I've completely stopped showing up to church or volunteering. At home, my family has had to start taking over tasks I used to manage because I just can't keep up anymore."
  }
]

However, because they describe rapidly swinging back and forth between high and low energy states over the past three months, F31.6 (mixed episode, which includes rapid alternation) also remains a strong candidate. Further clarification is needed regarding their state over the most recent week or two to determine if they are currently in a sustained depressive episode or still rapidly alternating.", "is_final": false}

But I do get very carried away with starting a million projects at once and taking on too many tasks, like tearing around the house reorganizing everything.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling consistently depressed and low, or have you been experiencing a rapid mix of both high-energy and low-mood states happening at the same time?\n\nPatient: I don't really feel them at the exact same time, but rather I swing back and forth between the two. I'll go from being in a high-energy, wired overdrive where I feel on top of the world and take on everything, to suddenly crashing into a heavy gray cloud where I feel useless, guilty, and completely exhausted. These unpredictable ups and downs have been going on like this for about three months now.\n\nDoctor: During the periods when you feel depressed and down, how much does it affect your ability to do daily activities like work, chores, or taking care of yourself—is it very difficult but you still manage, or has it become almost impossible to do anything?\n\nPatient: It has become incredibly difficult, almost impossible at times when that heavy gray cloud is over me. I've been written up at the warehouse for mistakes and missed shifts, and I've completely stopped showing up to church or volunteering. At home, my family has had to start taking over tasks I used to manage because I just can't keep up anymore.\n\nDoctor: When you swing between these high-energy and low-mood states, how quickly do these shifts happen? Do you switch back and forth within a few days or even hours, or does each state last for several weeks?"

These shifts have been happening back and forth over the last three months. It really varies—some days I feel incredibly wound up and on top of the world, while other days I crash into that heavy, useless feeling, rather than staying in one state for weeks on end without change.

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. It's gotten to the point where I just can't keep going like this.\n\nDoctor: Apart from your sleep issues, have you been experiencing persistent feelings of sadness, or a loss of interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, when my energy drops, a heavy gray cloud settles over me and I feel so useless and guilty about letting my family down. I've also completely lost interest in things I used to love, like knitting, volunteering at the food pantry, and going to church—it all just feels pointless now.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your mind is often racing at night. Do you find yourself constantly worrying about many different everyday things, and is this worry difficult to control?\n\nPatient: Yes, my mind is constantly running like a motor at night and I just can't seem to control it. My thoughts race from one thing to another—like doctor bills, my mom's health, and work tasks—piling on so fast that I can't grab hold of any single one of them.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant, hard-to-control worry about everyday things? It's actually been going on much longer than two weeks—it's been about three months now, ever since my daughter's divorce and my mom's health started sliding. I just feel so completely worn down and overwhelmed by it all.\n\nDoctor: During this difficult time, have you had any thoughts of death, or felt that you would be better off dead?\n\nPatient: Yes, there have been moments, especially after a bad argument at home or when I mess up at work, where I catch myself thinking it might be easier if I just didn't wake up. I get to feeling like my family would be better off without me dragging everyone down. I can easily get by on just three or four hours of sleep and still feel completely wired the next day.\n\nDoctor: When you experience these 'up' periods of high energy and needing very little sleep, how many days in a row do they typically last?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure of the exact number of days because I tend to lose track of time when I'm in that wired state. But it definitely feels like those high-energy spells last for several days at a time before I finally crash.\n\nDoctor: During these high-energy periods, have you ever experienced severe problems at work or home, or have you ever needed to be hospitalized because of them?\n\nPatient: I haven't been hospitalized, but I've definitely had severe problems at work and at home. They've even started taking over responsibilities I used to handle because of how unpredictable I've been.\n\nDoctor: During these high-energy periods, do you find yourself doing things that are risky or unusual for you, such as spending too much money, driving recklessly, or making impulsive decisions?\n\nPatient: I haven't really done specific things like driving recklessly or spending too much money. But I do get very carried away with starting a million projects at once and taking on too many tasks, like tearing around the house reorganizing everything.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling consistently depressed and low, or have you been experiencing a rapid mix of both high-energy and low-mood states happening at the same time?\n\nPatient: I don't really feel them at the exact same time, but rather I swing back and forth between the two. I'll go from being in a high-energy, wired overdrive where I feel on top of the world and take on everything, to suddenly crashing into a heavy gray cloud where I feel useless, guilty, and completely exhausted. These unpredictable ups and downs have been going on like this for about three months now.\n\nDoctor: During the periods when you feel depressed and down, how much does it affect your ability to do daily activities like work, chores, or taking care of yourself—is it very difficult but you still manage, or has it become almost impossible to do anything?\n\nPatient: It has become incredibly difficult, almost impossible at times when that heavy gray cloud is over me. I've been written up at the warehouse for mistakes and missed shifts, and I've completely stopped showing up to church or volunteering. At home, my family has had to start taking over tasks I used to manage because I just can't keep up anymore.\n\nDoctor: When you swing between these high-energy and low-mood states, how quickly do these shifts happen? Do you switch back and forth within a few days or even hours, or does each state last for several weeks?\n\nPatient: These shifts have been happening back and forth over the last three months. It really varies—some days I feel incredibly wound up and on top of the world, while other days I crash into that heavy, useless feeling, rather than staying in one state for weeks on end without change.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you been in a constant, unbroken state of deep depression, or have you continued to rapidly swing back and forth between high-energy and low-energy states?"

Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick). Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food). Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.
